118	
119	\subsection{Answer-Region Analysis}
120	\label{sec:answer-region}
121	
122	To address whether the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we analyzed the phase distribution of answer tokens. Across both models, 93.2\% of actual answer tokens (identified by \verb|\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \approx 1$). The PPL degradation proxy for answer tokens ($1.3$--$5.3{\times}$) is substantially lower than for reasoning-trace tokens ($5.7$--$43.5{\times}$) across all 16 conditions (paired Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). This suggests that while compound sparsity degrades intermediate reasoning quality, final answer tokens are partially shielded by their naturally focused attention patterns. We note this is proxy evidence based on phase membership rather than direct accuracy measurement; generation under compound sparsity conditions remains an important validation for future work. % exp_id: n1a_answer_region_analysis
123	
124	We provide a correlational mechanistic analysis in terms of discrimination collapse in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
125

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Jv1LexfestAb2rYHYHbZUt`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental-setup}
3	
4	\paragraph{Models.}
5	We evaluate on two 7B-parameter models sharing the Qwen2 backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA): \textbf{DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B}~\cite{deepseek-r1-2025}, distilled from DeepSeek-R1 with explicit chain-of-thought traces yielding pronounced phase structure; and \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct}~\cite{qwen25-2024}, with standard instruction tuning, providing a cross-model replication target. Both are loaded in \texttt{bfloat16} with eager attention.\footnote{An earlier \texttt{float16} configuration produced sporadic NaN values; \texttt{bfloat16} eliminated all occurrences.}
6	
7	\paragraph{Dataset.}
8	We evaluate all 500 problems from MATH-500~\cite{math500-neurips2021}. Each problem is decoded greedily (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048). Problems with degenerate phase assignments (null low or high phase) are excluded, yielding effective sample sizes of $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded). Qwen exhibits a higher mid-phase null rate (45.2\% vs.\ 10.2\% for DeepSeek) due to lower temporal autocorrelation in its attention sparsity patterns (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$, $d{=}-1.68$); this does not affect the primary gradient metric $\Delta = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high}}$, which requires only low and high phases. Sensitivity analysis across $\texttt{min\_phase\_length} \in \{5, 10, 20\}$ confirms robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Sparsity configuration.}
11	We apply symmetric compound sparsity at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, computing perplexity under four conditions: dense, attention-only, KV-only, and compound. We compare \emph{importance-based} eviction (position-normalized cumulative attention score, Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance}; BOS always retained) and \emph{random} eviction (uniform). Heads are pruned per layer by Frobenius norm; under random eviction, both heads and positions are selected uniformly.
12	
13	\paragraph{Phase segmentation and metrics.}
14	Each sequence is segmented into three phases using the quantile-based method (\S\ref{par:phase-segmentation}), with $\texttt{min\_phase\_length}{=}10$. We compute segment-level perplexity, II (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}), and $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}), reporting medians due to heavy right-skew (mean/median ratios up to 43:1). Robustness to segmentation granularity (quartile, quintile; Appendix~\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length ($5$, $10$, $20$ tokens; Appendix~\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}) is verified.
15	
16	\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}
17	\label{par:stats}
18	All reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-model), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is confirmed by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\rho{=}1.0$, exact permutation $p{=}0.042$, one-tailed) is consistent but has limited power at $n{=}4$ levels; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\times}$ across all 16 conditions, directly demonstrating precision gains. $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported as an auxiliary metric; the paired median difference $\Delta$ is primary.\footnote{Experiments on 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96\,GB GPUs.}
19	
```

```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	We present discrimination collapse as a \emph{correlational diagnostic} rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\widetilde{\mathrm{Gini}} \approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071
5	
6	\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}
7	\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}
8	
9	Head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071
10	
11	This pattern---which we term \emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
12	
13	To provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity
14	
15	We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
16	
17	\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}
18	\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}
19	
20	As a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---and is not a universal mechanism. Discrimination collapse remains the robust, model-independent correlate. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
21	
```

    "originalFile": "% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup\n% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.\n% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).\n\n\\section{Background and Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:background}\n\nWe formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}.\n\n\\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}\nLet $s \\in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \\emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \\emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \\emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \\emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \\cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \\emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \\cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \\cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Interaction Index.}\nFor a text segment, let $\\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \\in \\{d, a, k, b\\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \\text{PPL}_c / \\text{PPL}_d$, and:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.\n    \\label{eq:ii}\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null because the two axes operate on disjoint dimensions; qualitative conclusions hold under the multiplicative null (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). Because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed, we report medians throughout.\n\n\\paragraph{Severity gradient.}\nTo capture phase variation, the per-problem \\emph{amplification ratio} is:\n\\begin{equation}\n    G^{(i)} = \\frac{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low}}}{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{high}}},\n    \\label{eq:gradient}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \\widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}\n\\label{par:phase-segmentation}\nInspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \\texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation).     "newString": "amplification ratios ($G$) from $7.5{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$, 95\\% CI $[4.1, 11.4]$) to $79.8{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$, 95\\% CI $[63.3, 96.5]$; additive null; multiplicative null yields larger estimates) across reasoning phases",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nCompound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.\nWe show that compound severity is not phase-invariant: it is an emergent phase-strategy interaction with amplification ratios ($G$) from $7.5{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$, 95\\% CI $[4.1, 11.4]$) to $79.8{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$, 95\\% CI $[63.3, 96.5]$) across reasoning phases in mathematical reasoning models.\n\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems, $n_{\\text{eff}}{=}496$, $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$), importance-based eviction produces a monotonically increasing severity gradient from the high-sparsity to low-sparsity reasoning phase (median $\\Delta$: $0.39 \\to 8.48$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$; where significant, $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker), confirming importance-based eviction as the dominant driver.\nA crossover emerges at $s{=}0.25$: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$, $\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2-family architectures.\nWe present discrimination collapse---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a correlational diagnostic: both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops, yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.\nThese findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$; where significant, $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker), confirming importance-based eviction as the dominant driver.",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nCompound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.\nWe show that compound severity is not phase-invariant: it is an emergent phase-strategy interaction with amplification ratios ($G$) from $7.5{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$, 95\\% CI $[4.1, 11.4]$) to $79.8{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$, 95\\% CI $[63.3, 96.5]$; additive null; multiplicative null yields larger estimates) across reasoning phases in mathematical reasoning models.\n\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems, $n_{\\text{eff}}{=}496$, $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$), importance-based eviction produces a monotonically increasing severity gradient from the high-sparsity to low-sparsity reasoning phase (median $\\Delta$: $0.39 \\to 8.48$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$; where significant, $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker), confirming importance-based eviction as the dominant driver.\nA crossover emerges at $s{=}0.25$: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$, $\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2-family architectures.\nWe present discrimination collapse---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a correlational diagnostic: both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops, yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.\nThese findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "66097744-4f07-44bd-8bb0-b8967d32d0d0",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "06cc4077-8afe-434b-aceb-69b968484fc9",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "5ff93094-de1f-46bf-a055-356e1068fb1b",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$39{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS bonf_p_k4 all <1e-69; QW all <1e-14; DS rand s015/s020 ns, s025/s030 sig but 15-39x weaker; QW rand s015/s020 ns, s025/s030 sig but 2.7-20x weaker",
    "newString": "Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$17{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \\cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.\nTwo complementary responses---\\emph{attention head pruning} \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \\emph{KV cache eviction} \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---can be deployed simultaneously as \\emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \\cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.\n\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose generations alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.\nPhase-aware methods like ThinKV \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from greedy bias \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, deferred importance \\cite{retentivekv-2026}, and temporal dynamics in long generations \\cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple mechanisms are composed.\nIf compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWhy can't existing single-axis findings predict our results? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse on a single compression axis; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction without compound interaction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across generation stages. A linear combination of these insights would predict that compound degradation either (i) uniformly amplifies, or (ii) follows the dominant single-axis pattern. Instead, we observe a \\emph{severity gradient} spanning over an order of magnitude ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for importance vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ for random), an effect absent from any single-axis analysis. In DeepSeek, this further manifests as a strategy ranking crossover at $s{=}0.25$; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating the crossover is model-dependent. The compound interaction exceeds the multiplicative null by $2.7{\\times}$ in the low-sparsity phase while falling below it in the high-sparsity phase (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). This emergent coupling is not deducible from single-axis characterizations.\n\nWe show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems), the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$; Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$); preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) suggests the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 but at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with substantially weaker strategy contrast.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$39{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS bonf_p_k4 all <1e-69; QW all <1e-14; DS rand s015/s020 ns, s025/s030 sig but 15-39x weaker; QW rand s015/s020 ns, s025/s030 sig but 2.7-20x weaker\n\nWe present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{discrimination collapse}---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a contributing factor, with the \\emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning (which selectively removes high-score heads) as a key differentiator from random pruning.\n\nThis work is a \\textbf{diagnostic study}: we characterize a previously unknown interaction effect rather than proposing a mitigation method. Following the tradition of empirical analysis papers in NLP \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}, our contribution is establishing and quantifying the phenomenon of phase-modulated compound severity, providing measurement tools and diagnostic findings that inform future method design.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is $3$--$39{\\times}$ larger than under random eviction (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We show this gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two Qwen2-architecture models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), with exploratory evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We provide a \\textbf{diagnostic analysis} revealing that aggregate score collapse (Gini) is necessary but not sufficient for severity amplification, pointing to structural specificity of pruned heads as the key differentiator (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

This emergent coupling is not deducible from single-axis characterizations.\n\nWe show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems), the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$; Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$); preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) suggests the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 but at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with substantially weaker strategy contrast.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$17{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.\n\nWe present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{discrimination collapse}---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a contributing factor, with the \\emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning (which selectively removes high-score heads) as a key differentiator from random pruning.\n\nThis work is a \\textbf{diagnostic study}: we characterize a previously unknown interaction effect rather than proposing a mitigation method. Following the tradition of empirical analysis papers in NLP \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}, our contribution is establishing and quantifying the phenomenon of phase-modulated compound severity, providing measurement tools and diagnostic findings that inform future method design.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is $3$--$39{\\times}$ larger than under random eviction (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We show this gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two Qwen2-architecture models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), with exploratory evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We provide a \\textbf{diagnostic analysis} revealing that aggregate score collapse (Gini) is necessary but not sufficient for severity amplification, pointing to structural specificity of pruned heads as the key differentiator (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

{

  "old_string": "Why can't existing single-axis findings predict our results? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse on a single compression axis; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction without compound interaction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across generation stages. A linear combination of these insights would predict that compound degradation either (i) uniformly amplifies, or (ii) follows the dominant single-axis pattern. Instead, we observe a \\emph{severity gradient} spanning over an order of magnitude ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for importance vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ for random), an effect absent from any single-axis analysis. In DeepSeek, this further manifests as a strategy ranking crossover at $s{=}0.25$; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating the crossover is model-dependent. The compound interaction exceeds the multiplicative null by $2.7{\\times}$ in the low-sparsity phase while falling below it in the high-sparsity phase (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). This emergent coupling is not deducible from single-axis characterizations.",
  "new_string": "What does compound interaction add beyond single-axis findings? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across stages. Combining these insights qualitatively predicts phase-dependent compound degradation---but two quantitative patterns cannot be derived from single-axis analyses. First, the severity gradient follows a \\emph{strictly monotonic dose-response} across sparsity levels (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$), not merely a directional trend. Second, in DeepSeek a \\emph{strategy ranking crossover} emerges at $s{=}0.25$---importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases and underperforms in low-sparsity phases; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating model dependence. We report both the paired median difference $\\Delta$ ($0.39 \\to 8.48$) as the primary metric and the amplification ratio $G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ (importance) vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ (random) as an auxiliary measure; $G$ is amplified by near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ denominators, but $\\Delta$---which involves no ratios---shows the same monotonic gradient (\\S\\ref{sec:results})."
}

The compound interaction exceeds the multiplicative null by $2.7{\\times}$ in the low-sparsity phase while falling below it in the high-sparsity phase (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). This emergent coupling is not deducible from single-axis characterizations.",
    "newString": "What does compound interaction add beyond single-axis findings? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across stages. Combining these insights qualitatively predicts phase-dependent compound degradation---but two quantitative patterns cannot be derived from single-axis analyses. First, the severity gradient follows a \\emph{strictly monotonic dose-response} across sparsity levels (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$), not merely a directional trend. Second, in DeepSeek a \\emph{strategy ranking crossover} emerges at $s{=}0.25$---importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases and underperforms in low-sparsity phases; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating model dependence. We report both the paired median difference $\\Delta$ ($0.39 \\to 8.48$) as the primary metric and the amplification ratio $G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ (importance) vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ (random) as an auxiliary measure; $G$ is amplified by near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ denominators, but $\\Delta$---which involves no ratios---shows the same monotonic gradient (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \\cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.\nTwo complementary responses---\\emph{attention head pruning} \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \\emph{KV cache eviction} \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---can be deployed simultaneously as \\emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \\cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.\n\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose generations alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.\nPhase-aware methods like ThinKV \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from greedy bias \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, deferred importance \\cite{retentivekv-2026}, and temporal dynamics in long generations \\cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple mechanisms are composed.\nIf compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWhy can't existing single-axis findings predict our results? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse on a single compression axis; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction without compound interaction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across generation stages. A linear combination of these insights would predict that compound degradation either (i) uniformly amplifies, or (ii) follows the dominant single-axis pattern. Instead, we observe a \\emph{severity gradient} spanning over an order of magnitude ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ for importance vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ for random), an effect absent from any single-axis analysis. In DeepSeek, this further manifests as a strategy ranking crossover at $s{=}0.25$; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating the crossover is model-dependent. The compound interaction exceeds the multiplicative null by $2.7{\\times}$ in the low-sparsity phase while falling below it in the high-sparsity phase (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). This emergent coupling is not deducible from single-axis characterizations.\n\nWe show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems), the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$; Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$); preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) suggests the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 but at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with substantially weaker strategy contrast.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$17{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.\n\nWe present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{discrimination collapse}---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a contributing factor, with the \\emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning (which selectively removes high-score heads) as a key differentiator from random pruning.\n\nThis work is a \\textbf{diagnostic study}: we characterize a previously unknown interaction effect rather than proposing a mitigation method. Following the tradition of empirical analysis papers in NLP \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}, our contribution is establishing and quantifying the phenomenon of phase-modulated compound severity, providing measurement tools and diagnostic findings that inform future method design.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is $3$--$17{\\times}$ larger than under random eviction where both strategies show significant phase gradients ($s \\geq 0.25$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}).\\footnote{The full range including non-significant random conditions ($s \\leq 0.20$, where random $\\Delta$ CIs span zero) extends to $39{\\times}$.}\n    \\item We show this gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two Qwen2-architecture models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), with exploratory evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We provide a \\textbf{diagnostic analysis} revealing that aggregate score collapse (Gini) is necessary but not sufficient for severity amplification, pointing to structural specificity of pruned heads as the key differentiator (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

  "old_string": "To address whether the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we analyzed the phase distribution of answer tokens. Across both models, 93.2\\% of actual answer tokens (identified by \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \\approx 1$). The PPL degradation proxy for answer tokens ($1.3$--$5.3{\\times}$) is substantially lower than for reasoning-trace tokens ($5.7$--$43.5{\\times}$) across all 16 conditions (paired Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). This suggests that while compound sparsity degrades intermediate reasoning quality, final answer tokens are partially shielded by their naturally focused attention patterns. We note this is proxy evidence based on phase membership rather than direct accuracy measurement; generation under compound sparsity conditions remains an important validation for future work. % exp_id: n1a_answer_region_analysis",
  "new_string": "The phase-gated analysis reveals that compound severity is \\emph{non-uniform} across the generation, concentrating in specific functional regions. This non-uniformity is itself a key finding: it identifies \\emph{where} severity concentrates (reasoning trace) versus where it does not (final answer), providing a more precise signal than aggregate metrics for targeted compression strategies.\n\nAcross both models, 93.2\\% of actual answer tokens (identified by \\verb|\\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \\approx 1$). The PPL degradation proxy for answer tokens ($1.3$--$5.3{\\times}$) is substantially lower than for reasoning-trace tokens ($5.7$--$43.5{\\times}$) across all 16 conditions (paired Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). Critically, this does not imply that the severity gradient is inconsequential: reasoning-trace degradation may produce \\emph{silent reasoning errors}---outputs with correct formatting but corrupted intermediate logic---a failure mode that PPL can detect but answer-level accuracy may miss. We note this is proxy evidence based on phase membership rather than direct accuracy measurement; generation under compound sparsity conditions remains an important validation for future work."
}

```text
12	
13	\paragraph{Perplexity vs.\ accuracy.}
14	Our severity metric is perplexity-based (II), which measures next-token prediction degradation and has a known nonlinear relationship with downstream accuracy. We chose perplexity because its per-token granularity enables the phase-level decomposition central to our analysis; accuracy is binary per-problem and cannot be meaningfully decomposed by reasoning phase. Future work should investigate problem-level accuracy regression on phase-specific II to quantify how the perplexity-level severity gradient translates to task performance. Even if final answers survive due to natural phase shielding (\S\ref{sec:answer-region}), degraded intermediate reasoning may affect reliability on harder problems, reduce interpretability, and impact open-ended reasoning tasks where no structured answer format provides natural shielding.
15	
16	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
17	Our experiments span $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics. We do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization. Extended limitations in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}.
18	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\paragraph{Model scale and architecture.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7--8B parameter scale. Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested. Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) partially confirms the qualitative pattern beyond the Qwen2 architecture---monotonically increasing $d_z$ with within-importance phase gradient $\\sim$1.0---but with substantially weaker interaction magnitude ($\\sim$1/40 of DeepSeek) and mixed statistical evidence (only $s{=}0.30$ significant after Bonferroni correction). The pattern has been tested on two architecture families (Qwen2ForCausalLM, LlamaForCausalLM); comprehensive validation across more diverse architectures (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention) and larger scales is needed for full generalization claims.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark. MATH-500 is a suitable first testbed because it elicits structured reasoning with clear phase transitions (exploratory vs.\\ structured computation), supports per-problem evaluation enabling paired statistical tests, and spans sufficient difficulty to produce meaningful variation in sequence length and attention dynamics. However, different reasoning tasks---code generation (e.g., HumanEval, LiveCodeBench), science reasoning (e.g., GPQA), multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile. Whether the phase-strategy interaction documented here extends to these domains is an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric sparsity assumption.}\nOur analysis applies symmetric sparsity rates: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. Practical deployments may use asymmetric budgets (e.g., aggressive head pruning with conservative cache eviction, or vice versa). The interaction dynamics under asymmetric compound sparsity may differ from the symmetric case studied here; investigating asymmetric compound interactions is a natural extension.\n\n\\paragraph{Mechanism.}\nThe discrimination collapse mechanism is supported by correlational evidence (consistent Gini/CV reduction across all conditions); causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation is needed. The entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2/4 Qwen conditions, suggesting model-specific factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Perplexity vs.\\ accuracy.}\nOur severity metric is perplexity-based (II), which measures next-token prediction degradation and has a known nonlinear relationship with downstream accuracy. ```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053
12	
13	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
14	At $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \geq 0.25$ (DS: $\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913
15	
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Characterization as diagnostic signal.}\nThe primary value of the phase-dependent severity gradient is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it identifies \\emph{where} compound sparsity is most destructive within a generation. The monotonically increasing $G$ across sparsity levels provides a natural signal for allocating compression budgets---phases with $G \\gg 1$ are precisely those where uniform budgets incur order-of-magnitude penalties. The pilot experiment below confirms the direction is correct (Wilcoxon-significant recovery) but the effect size is insufficient under coarse phase boundaries, suggesting that effective mitigation requires finer-grained phase-aware optimization. We position mitigation as an open problem for which our characterization provides the necessary foundation: future work can leverage the severity gradient as an objective for joint optimization of budget allocation and strategy selection.\n\nA preliminary pilot experiment explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases. At $s{=}0.25$, oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery ($p = 0.024$), but 95\\% CIs for both conditions span zero. % exp_id: c5b_pilot; oracle 18%, heuristic 32%, gate FAIL These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases---a promising direction for future work.\n\nExtended discussion in Appendix~\\ref{app:extended-discussion}.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Characterization as diagnostic signal.}\nThe primary value of the phase-dependent severity gradient is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it identifies \\emph{where} compound sparsity is most destructive within a generation and characterizes phase-dependent sensitivity patterns that inform future compression strategy design. Preliminary mitigation experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:pilot-mitigation}) yielded limited recovery (oracle 18\\%), suggesting that phase-gated severity signals alone are insufficient for direct compression budget optimization---additional mechanisms (e.g., strategy switching, adaptive thresholding) warrant investigation. We position mitigation as an open problem for which our characterization provides the necessary diagnostic foundation.\n\nExtended discussion in Appendix~\\ref{app:extended-discussion}."
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018W6ZDCyyCy5SFMLbJFqfnr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053
12	
13	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
14	At $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \geq 0.25$ (DS: $\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913
15	
16	\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}
17	Directly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.
18	
19	
20	\section{Extended Limitations}
21	\label{app:limitations}
22	
23	\paragraph{Model scale.}
24	All experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.
25	Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.
26	Larger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).
27	
28	
29	\paragraph{Architectural scope.}
30	Our two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
31	The cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
32	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.
33	
34	
35	\paragraph{Task coverage.}
36	We evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.
37	Different reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.
38	Generalizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.
39	
40	
41	\paragraph{Statistical scope.}
42	Bootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest
43	The severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final
44	All four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]
45	
46	At $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]
47	These effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.
48	
49	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
50	Our experiments span $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.
51	More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.
52	Conversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.
53	
54	
55	\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}
56	We do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.
57	
58	% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \S\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}
59	
```

</details>

Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",
      "numLines": 59,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 59
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Tt9AyAswzb4ah3Nhnu7cNg`</summary>

```text
7	\paragraph{Implications for deployment.}
8	Applying a uniform compression budget across all inference steps implicitly accepts order-of-magnitude severity penalties during low-sparsity reasoning phases. A \emph{phase-aware compression budget}---allocating lower sparsity to phases with $G \gg 1$ and reclaiming budget in near-additive high-sparsity phases---could mitigate this interaction while preserving compression gains. The severity gradient provides a natural signal for such allocation. We expect qualitatively similar behavior for other attention-mass-based scoring methods (H$_2$O \cite{h2o-neurips2023}, SnapKV \cite{snapkv-neurips2024}, PyramidKV \cite{pyramidkv-colm2025}), as they share the property that head pruning alters the score distribution feeding into eviction decisions; however, quantitative magnitudes may differ given implementation-specific scoring mechanisms.
9	
10	\paragraph{Characterization as diagnostic signal.}
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Quantitative surprise.}\nWhile the qualitative direction---importance scores degrade when attention is diffuse---may seem expected, the quantitative structure is not predictable from first principles. The severity gradient ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ at $n{=}496$) far exceeds a naive ``somewhat worse'' prediction. The crossover on DeepSeek---where importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}} = 0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) and underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$)---cannot be derived from single-axis entropy reasoning. % exp_id: crossover_all_sparsity.csv; DS s025 n=496 The near-unity random baseline ($G{=}0.5$--$1.3{\\times}$, trivial magnitude) confirms the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for deployment.}\nApplying a uniform compression budget across all inference steps implicitly accepts order-of-magnitude severity penalties during low-sparsity reasoning phases. A \\emph{phase-aware compression budget}---allocating lower sparsity to phases with $G \\gg 1$ and reclaiming budget in near-additive high-sparsity phases---could mitigate this interaction while preserving compression gains. The severity gradient provides a natural signal for such allocation. We expect qualitatively similar behavior for other attention-mass-based scoring methods (H$_2$O \\cite{h2o-neurips2023}, SnapKV \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024}, PyramidKV \\cite{pyramidkv-colm2025}), as they share the property that head pruning alters the score distribution feeding into eviction decisions; however, quantitative magnitudes may differ given implementation-specific scoring mechanisms.\n\n\\paragraph{Characterization as diagnostic signal.}\nThe primary value of the phase-dependent severity gradient is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it identifies \\emph{where} compound sparsity is most destructive within a generation and characterizes phase-dependent sensitivity patterns that inform future compression strategy design. Preliminary mitigation experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:pilot-mitigation}) yielded limited recovery (oracle 18\\%), suggesting that phase-gated severity signals alone are insufficient for direct compression budget optimization---additional mechanisms (e.g., strategy switching, adaptive thresholding) warrant investigation. We position mitigation as an open problem for which our characterization provides the necessary diagnostic foundation.\n\nExtended discussion in Appendix~\\ref{app:extended-discussion}.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture-dependent magnitude.}\nCross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) provides partial support for the qualitative pattern---monotonically increasing severity gradient under importance-based eviction with within-strategy phase gradient $d_z \\geq 1.0$---while revealing that absolute severity magnitudes are architecture-dependent (${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek, ${\\sim}7{\\times}$ smaller than Qwen) and the strategy--phase interaction is substantially weaker than in Qwen2-based architectures. Notably, random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA ($d_z$: $0.35 \\to 0.44$), reducing the contrast between strategies. % exp_id: c3_llama; random phase_gradient d_z from c3_llama_results.json This dissociation between qualitatively consistent directional trends and markedly different magnitudes suggests the phase-strategy interaction may be a general property of compound sparsity on reasoning models, while its quantitative impact---and the degree to which it is strategy-specific---depends on architecture-specific attention distributions and head specialization patterns. % exp_id: c3_llama\n",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "We present discrimination collapse as a \\emph{correlational diagnostic} rather than a complete causal explanation.",
    "newString": "The mechanisms discussed here are \\emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. We present discrimination collapse as one such diagnostic rather than a complete causal explanation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nWe present discrimination collapse as a \\emph{correlational diagnostic} rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \\emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{Gini}} \\approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071\n\n\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nHead pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \\emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071\n\nThis pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nTo provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity\n\nWe note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nAs a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---and is not a universal mechanism. Discrimination collapse remains the robust, model-independent correlate. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

```text
82	
83	\subsection{Replication Across Training Paradigms}
84	\label{sec:cross-model}
85	
86	DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, enabling controlled comparison of training paradigm effects.
87	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \S\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat applies to mid-phase comparisons: on Qwen, 45.2\% of problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation in attention sparsity (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\ DeepSeek's $0.894$; $d{=}-1.68$), preventing formation of contiguous mid-phase runs above the $\texttt{min\_phase\_length}{=}10$ threshold. This does not affect the primary low--high contrast ($\Delta$). Per-phase effective sample sizes are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}. Full cross-model tables appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW imp Δ 0.07→3.16, all p<1e-14; QW rand s025/s030 sig but 2.7-20x weaker; mid-null: qwen_midphase_null_analysis.md
```

          " ",
          " DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, enabling controlled comparison of training paradigm effects.",
          "-Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \\geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \\S\\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat applies to mid-phase comparisons: on Qwen, 45.2\\% of problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation in attention sparsity (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\\ DeepSeek's $0.894$; $d{=}-1.68$), preventing formation of contiguous mid-phase runs above the $\\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}{=}10$ threshold. This does not affect the primary low--high contrast ($\\Delta$). Per-phase effective sample sizes are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}. Full cross-model tables appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-details}. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW imp Δ 0.07→3.16, all p<1e-14; QW rand s025/s030 sig but 2.7-20x weaker; mid-null: qwen_midphase_null_analysis.md",
          "+Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude.
