```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "6fc4a4d9-15ce-4070-a37c-37e8071ac3cf",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "06cc4077-8afe-434b-aceb-69b968484fc9",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "ec3b407b-2b80-4446-ae70-a11e54bfb2e4",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "The cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.",
    "newString": "The cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases. At $s{=}0.25$, oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery ($p = 0.024$), but 95\\% CIs for both conditions span zero. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.",

All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. All importance-based $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; ``ns'' denotes $p_{\\text{Bonf}} > 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:mpl-sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Strategy & $s$ & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$5} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$10} & \\multicolumn{1}{c}{mpl$=$20} & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(10) & $n_{\\text{eff}}$(20) \\\\\n\\midrule\nDS & Imp & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.391 & 0.393 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.215 & 1.235 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.25 & 3.986 & 3.986 & 4.017 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Imp & 0.30 & 8.481 & 8.481 & 8.420 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nDS & Rand & 0.15 & 0.014 & 0.014\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.012\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.20 & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.031\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.023\\textsuperscript{ns} & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.25 & 0.257 & 0.257 & 0.190 & 496 & 433 \\\\\nDS & Rand & 0.30 & 0.510 & 0.510 & 0.410 & 496 & 433 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Imp & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 0.066 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.307 & 0.325 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.074 & 1.106 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Imp & 0.30 & 3.155 & 3.155 & 3.581 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQW & Rand & 0.15 & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & $-$0.011\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.20 & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 0.015\\textsuperscript{ns} & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.25 & 0.393 & 0.393 & 0.266 & 475 & 289 \\\\\nQW & Rand & 0.30 & 0.913 & 0.913 & 0.788 & 475 & 289 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe main effect of increasing mpl is reduced $n_{\\text{eff}}$: at mpl$=$20, DeepSeek retains 433/500 problems and Qwen retains 289/500 (mid-null rate rises to 71.2\\% and 84.6\\%, respectively). Despite this sample reduction, all significance conclusions hold. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; source: min_phase_length_sensitivity.csv\n\n\n\\section{Segmentation Robustness}\n\\label{app:seg-robust}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seg-robust} reports the paired median difference $\\Delta$ under tercile, quartile, and quintile segmentation for both models under importance-based eviction. Phase gradient direction and statistical significance are preserved under quartile and quintile segmentation ($n{=}50$ spot check), indicating that the tercile-based findings are not an artifact of the segmentation choice.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Segmentation robustness: median $\\Delta$ under tercile ($n{=}496$/$475$), quartile ($n{=}50$ spot check), and quintile ($n{=}50$ spot check) segmentation. Importance-based eviction; all $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-7}$.}\n\\label{tab:seg-robust}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & Tercile & Quartile\\; [95\\% CI] & Quintile\\; [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{DeepSeek} & 0.15 & 0.391 & 0.491\\; $[0.44,\\; 0.70]$ & 0.505\\; $[0.33,\\; 0.71]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 1.215 & 1.388\\; $[1.05,\\; 1.79]$ & 1.197\\; $[1.02,\\; 1.76]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 3.986 & 4.322\\; $[3.62,\\; 8.06]$ & 4.960\\; $[3.80,\\; 9.02]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 8.481 & 12.930\\; $[9.83,\\; 19.10]$ & 11.548\\; $[10.06,\\; 18.51]$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen} & 0.15 & 0.066 & 0.226\\; $[0.14,\\; 0.27]$ & 0.203\\; $[0.13,\\; 0.26]$ \\\\\n& 0.20 & 0.307 & 0.561\\; $[0.35,\\; 0.77]$ & 0.517\\; $[0.29,\\; 0.75]$ \\\\\n& 0.25 & 1.074 & 1.870\\; $[1.05,\\; 2.44]$ & 1.801\\; $[1.13,\\; 2.38]$ \\\\\n& 0.30 & 3.155 & 4.097\\; $[2.91,\\; 4.94]$ & 4.009\\; $[3.28,\\; 5.34]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe quartile and quintile $\\Delta$ values are comparable to or slightly larger than tercile values because finer-grained segmentation isolates more extreme phases (the ``lowest'' quintile captures a more concentrated low-sparsity segment than the lowest tercile). The monotonic dose-response ($\\Delta$ increasing with $s$) is preserved across all three segmentation schemes for both models, confirming that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the tercile segmentation choice. % exp_id: segmentation_sensitivity.csv\n\n\n\\section{Outlier Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:outlier-robustness}\n\nThe extreme mean/median ratio (up to $97{,}640{:}1$ in individual conditions) reflects heavy-tailed II distributions driven by near-zero denominators in specific problems. We verify robustness via symmetric winsorization at $5\\%$ and $10\\%$ levels. All median-based statistics---paired median difference $\\Delta$, Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$-values, and amplification ratio $G$---are strictly invariant under winsorization, as expected for rank-based estimators. The five core claims---compound exceeds individual, importance exceeds random, monotonic dose-response, $G \\gg 1$ for importance, $G \\approx 1$ for random---are fully robust. The extreme outlier severity further validates our choice of median-based inference over mean-based alternatives. $G$ values in this robustness analysis follow the ratio-of-medians convention (Table~\\ref{tab:profiling-subset}); the primary $G$ definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is similarly invariant as a median of per-problem ratios. % exp_id: w6_outlier_robustness\n",

```text
86	DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, enabling controlled comparison of training paradigm effects.
87	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \S\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat applies to mid-phase comparisons: on Qwen, 45.2\% of problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation in attention sparsity (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\ DeepSeek's $0.894$; $d{=}-1.68$), preventing formation of contiguous mid-phase runs above the $\texttt{min\_phase\_length}{=}10$ threshold. This does not affect the primary low--high contrast ($\Delta$). Per-phase effective sample sizes are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}. Full cross-training-paradigm tables appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW imp Δ 0.07→3.16, all p<1e-14; QW rand s025/s030 sig but 2.7-20x weaker; mid-null: qwen_midphase_null_analysis.md
88	
89	\subsection{Exploratory Cross-Architecture Analysis}
90	\label{sec:cross-arch}
91	
92	Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is the profiling subset; full-scale ($n{=}500$) replication on LlamaForCausalLM was deprioritized in favor of the primary Qwen2 analysis.} confirms the phase-gated severity pattern for importance-based eviction (post-hoc power $> 0.99$ for within-importance gradient) but lacks statistical power ($0.26$--$0.63$) for strategy comparisons; the interaction magnitude is ${\sim}40{\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek. Full results, including per-sparsity breakdowns and a power analysis motivating the sample size needed for definitive cross-architecture claims, appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}. % exp_id: c3_llama
93	
```

{

    "oldString": "providing a cross-model replication target",
    "newString": "providing a cross-training-paradigm replication target",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental-setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nWe evaluate on two 7B-parameter models sharing the Qwen2 backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA): \\textbf{DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B}~\\cite{deepseek-r1-2025}, distilled from DeepSeek-R1 with explicit chain-of-thought traces yielding pronounced phase structure; and \\textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct}~\\cite{qwen25-2024}, with standard instruction tuning, providing a cross-model replication target. Both are loaded in \\texttt{bfloat16} with eager attention.\\footnote{An earlier \\texttt{float16} configuration produced sporadic NaN values; \\texttt{bfloat16} eliminated all occurrences.}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset.}\nWe evaluate all 500 problems from MATH-500~\\cite{math500-neurips2021}. Each problem is decoded greedily (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048). Problems with degenerate phase assignments (null low or high phase) are excluded, yielding effective sample sizes of $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded). Qwen exhibits a higher mid-phase null rate (45.2\\% vs.\\ 10.2\\% for DeepSeek) due to lower temporal autocorrelation in its attention sparsity patterns (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\\ $0.894$, $d{=}-1.68$); this does not affect the primary gradient metric $\\Delta = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$, which requires only low and high phases. Sensitivity analysis across $\\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} \\in \\{5, 10, 20\\}$ confirms robustness (Appendix~\\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity configuration.}\nWe apply symmetric compound sparsity at $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, computing perplexity under four conditions: dense, attention-only, KV-only, and compound. We compare \\emph{importance-based} eviction (position-normalized cumulative attention score, Eq.~\\ref{eq:kv-importance}; BOS always retained) and \\emph{random} eviction (uniform). Heads are pruned per layer by Frobenius norm; under random eviction, both heads and positions are selected uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase segmentation and metrics.}\nEach sequence is segmented into three phases using the quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}), with $\\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}{=}10$. We compute segment-level perplexity, II (Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}), and $G$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}), reporting medians due to heavy right-skew (mean/median ratios up to 43:1). Robustness to segmentation granularity (quartile, quintile; Appendix~\\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length ($5$, $10$, $20$ tokens; Appendix~\\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}) is verified.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}\n\\label{par:stats}\nAll reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-model), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is confirmed by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\\rho{=}1.0$, exact permutation $p{=}0.042$, one-tailed) is consistent but has limited power at $n{=}4$ levels; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\\times}$ across all 16 conditions, directly demonstrating precision gains. $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported as an auxiliary metric; the paired median difference $\\Delta$ is primary.\\footnote{Experiments on 4$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96\\,GB GPUs.}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011FppUXfPj7BUfuufQN1d93`</summary>

```text
16	\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}
17	\label{par:stats}
18	All reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-model), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is confirmed by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\rho{=}1.0$, exact permutation $p{=}0.042$, one-tailed) is consistent but has limited power at $n{=}4$ levels; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\times}$ across all 16 conditions, directly demonstrating precision gains. $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported as an auxiliary metric; the paired median difference $\Delta$ is primary.\footnote{Experiments on 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96\,GB GPUs.}
19	
```

Both are loaded in \\texttt{bfloat16} with eager attention.\\footnote{An earlier \\texttt{float16} configuration produced sporadic NaN values; \\texttt{bfloat16} eliminated all occurrences.}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset.}\nWe evaluate all 500 problems from MATH-500~\\cite{math500-neurips2021}. Each problem is decoded greedily (\\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens}$=$2048). Problems with degenerate phase assignments (null low or high phase) are excluded, yielding effective sample sizes of $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded). Qwen exhibits a higher mid-phase null rate (45.2\\% vs.\\ 10.2\\% for DeepSeek) due to lower temporal autocorrelation in its attention sparsity patterns (lag-1 $r{=}0.718$ vs.\\ $0.894$, $d{=}-1.68$); this does not affect the primary gradient metric $\\Delta = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low}} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$, which requires only low and high phases. Sensitivity analysis across $\\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length} \\in \\{5, 10, 20\\}$ confirms robustness (Appendix~\\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity configuration.}\nWe apply symmetric compound sparsity at $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, computing perplexity under four conditions: dense, attention-only, KV-only, and compound. We compare \\emph{importance-based} eviction (position-normalized cumulative attention score, Eq.~\\ref{eq:kv-importance}; BOS always retained) and \\emph{random} eviction (uniform). Heads are pruned per layer by Frobenius norm; under random eviction, both heads and positions are selected uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase segmentation and metrics.}\nEach sequence is segmented into three phases using the quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}), with $\\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}{=}10$. We compute segment-level perplexity, II (Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}), and $G$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}), reporting medians due to heavy right-skew (mean/median ratios up to 43:1). Robustness to segmentation granularity (quartile, quintile; Appendix~\\ref{app:seg-robust}) and minimum phase length ($5$, $10$, $20$ tokens; Appendix~\\ref{app:mpl-sensitivity}) is verified.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}\n\\label{par:stats}\nAll reported confidence intervals use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed$=$42)~\\cite{efron-1993}. Significance tests use Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired within-model) or Mann-Whitney $U$ (cross-model), with Bonferroni correction at family-wise $k{=}4$ (per model$\\times$strategy combination, 4 sparsity levels testing one dose-response hypothesis). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 tests is reported as a robustness check in Appendix~\\ref{app:bh-fdr}; all Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR. Dose-response monotonicity is confirmed by the Jonckheere-Terpstra test ($z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), which uses per-problem values (${\\sim}496$ per group) rather than aggregated medians.\\footnote{Spearman rank correlation on the four aggregate medians ($\\rho{=}1.0$, exact permutation $p{=}0.042$, one-tailed) is consistent but has limited power at $n{=}4$ levels; we rely on the JT test as primary evidence.} Scaling from $n{=}50$ (profiling) to $n{=}500$ narrowed bootstrap CI widths by $2.5$--$5.9{\\times}$ across all 16 conditions, directly demonstrating precision gains. $G$ (amplification ratio, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) is reported as an auxiliary metric; the paired median difference $\\Delta$ is primary.\\footnote{Experiments on 4$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96\\,GB GPUs.}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup\n% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.\n% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).\n\n\\section{Background and Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:background}\n\nWe formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}.\n\n\\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}\nLet $s \\in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \\emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \\emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \\emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \\emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random \\cite{nacl-acl2024}. Applying both simultaneously yields \\emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \\cite{spark-aaai2026}. Existing joint-pruning approaches such as GRASPrune \\cite{graspune-acl2026} learn global budget-constrained gates but treat decisions as static across generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Interaction Index.}\nFor a text segment, let $\\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \\in \\{d, a, k, b\\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \\text{PPL}_c / \\text{PPL}_d$, and:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.\n    \\label{eq:ii}\n\\end{equation}\n$\\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null ($\\mathrm{II} = 0$ under no interaction) as the primary formulation. Although the two sparsity axes are causally coupled---head pruning alters the importance scores that KV eviction relies on (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})---the additive null provides the most \\emph{conservative} baseline: it assumes compound loss equals the sum of individual losses ($r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$), the weakest independence assumption. If even this permissive null is rejected ($\\mathrm{II} > 0$), the interaction is super-additive regardless of null specification. Appendix~\\ref{app:multiplicative-null} reports parallel results under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \\cdot r_k$), which yields larger amplification estimates ($G_{\\times} = 58$--$241{\\times}$), confirming that our conclusions are null-choice invariant. Because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed, we report medians throughout.\n\n\\paragraph{Severity gradient.}\nTo capture phase variation, the per-problem \\emph{amplification ratio} is:\n\\begin{equation}\n    G^{(i)} = \\frac{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low}}}{\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{high}}},\n    \\label{eq:gradient}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\\,\\textit{low/high}}$ is the interaction index in the low/high-sparsity phase for problem $i$. We report $G = \\widetilde{G^{(i)}}$, the median across problems, with bootstrap CIs. We use the median of per-problem ratios (not the ratio of medians) to preserve per-problem pairing; see Appendix~\\ref{app:profiling-subset} for comparison. $G \\gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}\n\\label{par:phase-segmentation}\nInspired by ThinKV's phase-aware perspective on reasoning-time compression~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}, each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, using a quantile-based segmentation rather than explicit reasoning delimiters (e.g., \\texttt{&lt;think>} tokens). Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). Details in Appendix~\\ref{app:formulation-details}.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WP6AtCWPuFFV6noY9d6Wf7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Compound compression.}
5	Combining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.
6	\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.
7	\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.
8	\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.
9	GRASPrune~\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.
10	PM-KVQ~\cite{pmkvq-iclr2026} demonstrates that progressive quantization---gradually reducing bit-width over the generation process---mitigates cumulative error in long-CoT reasoning, implicitly introducing a temporal dimension to compression severity that resonates with our finding that compound interaction is phase-dependent.
11	These works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.
12	
13	\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}
14	Most methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.
15	Ada-KV~\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.
16	\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.
17	RetentiveKV~\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.
18	\cite{hies-2025} independently show that importance-based head discrimination collapses under heavy single-axis pruning. Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\S\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.
19	RLKV~\cite{rlkv-2025} uses reinforcement learning to identify reasoning-critical heads and selectively preserve their KV caches, demonstrating that head-level importance varies significantly for reasoning tasks. LongFlow~\cite{longflow-2026} targets the long-output setting of reasoning models with an efficient importance metric derived from attention intermediates.
20	Lethe~\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.
21	MUSTAFAR~\cite{mustafar-neurips2025} applies multi-stage adaptive compression for reasoning, while LKV~\cite{lkv-2026} learns end-to-end head-wise budgets for KV cache allocation.
22	Concurrent work examines KV cache compression's impact on reasoning capabilities \citep{hold-onto-thought-neurips2025}, evaluating single-axis compression strategies on mathematical reasoning benchmarks; our work extends this analysis to the compound setting where head pruning and KV eviction interact.
23	These methods each optimize a single pruning axis---whether aggregation, metric, temporal, or channel---without characterizing compound interactions when multiple mechanisms are simultaneously active.
24	
25	\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}
26	ThinKV~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.
27	\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\ ``decisive'' representation phases.
28	\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.
29	\cite{physics-kvcache-2026} identify a sharp phase transition in single-axis KV cache compression at high sparsity, characterizing the single-dimension failure mode that our compound analysis extends across multiple axes.
30	R-KV~\cite{rkv-neurips2025} exploits reasoning-specific redundancy for aggressive cache compression, targeting per-axis optimization rather than characterizing compound degradation.
31	These works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.
32	Our work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.
33	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Compound compression.}\nCombining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.\n\\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \\cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.\n\\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.\n\\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.\nGRASPrune~\\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.\nPM-KVQ~\\cite{pmkvq-iclr2026} demonstrates that progressive quantization---gradually reducing bit-width over the generation process---mitigates cumulative error in long-CoT reasoning, implicitly introducing a temporal dimension to compression severity that resonates with our finding that compound interaction is phase-dependent.\n% TODO: ThinK (channel-level compound compression), Prune-then-Quantize (compression order interaction) citations pending Agent verification\nThese works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.\n\n\\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}\nMost methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.\nAda-KV~\\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \\cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.\n\\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.\nRetentiveKV~\\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.\n\\cite{hies-2025} independently show that importance-based head discrimination collapses under heavy single-axis pruning. Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.\nRLKV~\\cite{rlkv-2025} uses reinforcement learning to identify reasoning-critical heads and selectively preserve their KV caches, demonstrating that head-level importance varies significantly for reasoning tasks. LongFlow~\\cite{longflow-2026} targets the long-output setting of reasoning models with an efficient importance metric derived from attention intermediates.\nLethe~\\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.\nMUSTAFAR~\\cite{mustafar-neurips2025} applies multi-stage adaptive compression for reasoning, while LKV~\\cite{lkv-2026} learns end-to-end head-wise budgets for KV cache allocation.\nConcurrent work examines KV cache compression's impact on reasoning capabilities \\citep{hold-onto-thought-neurips2025}, evaluating single-axis compression strategies on mathematical reasoning benchmarks; our work extends this analysis to the compound setting where head pruning and KV eviction interact.\nThese methods each optimize a single pruning axis---whether aggregation, metric, temporal, or channel---without characterizing compound interactions when multiple mechanisms are simultaneously active.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}\nThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.\n\\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\\ ``decisive'' representation phases.\n\\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.\n\\cite{physics-kvcache-2026} identify a sharp phase transition in single-axis KV cache compression at high sparsity, characterizing the single-dimension failure mode that our compound analysis extends across multiple axes.\nR-KV~\\cite{rkv-neurips2025} exploits reasoning-specific redundancy for aggressive cache compression, targeting per-axis optimization rather than characterizing compound degradation.\nThese works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.\nOur work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nCompound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.\nWe show that compound severity is not phase-invariant: it is an emergent phase-strategy interaction with amplification ratios ($G$) from $7.5{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$, 95\\% CI $[4.1, 11.4]$) to $79.8{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$, 95\\% CI $[63.3, 96.5]$; additive null; multiplicative null yields larger estimates) across reasoning phases in mathematical reasoning models.\n\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems, $n_{\\text{eff}}{=}496$, $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$), importance-based eviction produces a monotonically increasing severity gradient from the high-sparsity to low-sparsity reasoning phase (median $\\Delta$: $0.39 \\to 8.48$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ (non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$; where significant, $3$--$17{\\times}$ weaker), confirming importance-based eviction as the dominant driver.\nA crossover emerges at $s{=}0.25$: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}}{=}475$, $\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2-family architectures.\nWe present discrimination collapse---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a correlational diagnostic: both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops, yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.\nThese findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "3c68446a-90fd-4038-a44b-df65cc024f18",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "06cc4077-8afe-434b-aceb-69b968484fc9",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "bdc27b26-e5c1-44af-93bb-5294c7699d12",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "We present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{discrimination collapse}---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a contributing factor",
    "newString": "We present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{importance score flattening}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a contributing factor",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \\cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.\nTwo complementary responses---\\emph{attention head pruning} \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \\emph{KV cache eviction} \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---can be deployed simultaneously as \\emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \\cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.\n\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose generations alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.\nPhase-aware methods like ThinKV \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from greedy bias \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, deferred importance \\cite{retentivekv-2026}, and temporal dynamics in long generations \\cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple mechanisms are composed.\nIf compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWhat does compound interaction add beyond single-axis findings? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across stages. Combining these insights qualitatively predicts phase-dependent compound degradation---but two quantitative patterns cannot be derived from single-axis analyses. First, the severity gradient follows a \\emph{strictly monotonic dose-response} across sparsity levels (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$), not merely a directional trend. Second, in DeepSeek a \\emph{strategy ranking crossover} emerges at $s{=}0.25$---importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases and underperforms in low-sparsity phases; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating model dependence. We report both the paired median difference $\\Delta$ ($0.39 \\to 8.48$) as the primary metric and the amplification ratio $G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ (importance) vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ (random) as an auxiliary measure; $G$ is amplified by near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ denominators, but $\\Delta$---which involves no ratios---shows the same monotonic gradient (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\nWe show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems), the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$; Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$); preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) suggests the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 but at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with substantially weaker strategy contrast.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$17{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.\n\nWe present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{discrimination collapse}---head pruning flattening cumulative importance scores---as a contributing factor, with the \\emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning (which selectively removes high-score heads) as a key differentiator from random pruning.\n\nThis work is a \\textbf{diagnostic study}: we characterize a previously unknown interaction effect rather than proposing a mitigation method. Following the tradition of empirical analysis papers in NLP \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}, our contribution is establishing and quantifying the phenomenon of phase-modulated compound severity, providing measurement tools and diagnostic findings that inform future method design.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is $3$--$17{\\times}$ larger than under random eviction where both strategies show significant phase gradients ($s \\geq 0.25$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}).\\footnote{The full range including non-significant random conditions ($s \\leq 0.20$, where random $\\Delta$ CIs span zero) extends to $39{\\times}$.}\n    \\item We show this gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two Qwen2-architecture models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), with exploratory evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We provide a \\textbf{diagnostic analysis} revealing that aggregate score collapse (Gini) is necessary but not sufficient for severity amplification, pointing to structural specificity of pruned heads as the key differentiator (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \\cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.\nTwo complementary responses---\\emph{attention head pruning} \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \\emph{KV cache eviction} \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---can be deployed simultaneously as \\emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \\cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.\n\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose generations alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.\nPhase-aware methods like ThinKV \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from greedy bias \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, deferred importance \\cite{retentivekv-2026}, and temporal dynamics in long generations \\cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple mechanisms are composed.\nIf compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWhat does compound interaction add beyond single-axis findings? HIES~\\cite{hies-2025} characterizes score collapse; ThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} introduces phase-aware eviction; \\citet{sparse-frontier-2025} document strategy non-dominance across stages. Combining these insights qualitatively predicts phase-dependent compound degradation---but two quantitative patterns cannot be derived from single-axis analyses. First, the severity gradient follows a \\emph{strictly monotonic dose-response} across sparsity levels (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$), not merely a directional trend. Second, in DeepSeek a \\emph{strategy ranking crossover} emerges at $s{=}0.25$---importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases and underperforms in low-sparsity phases; Qwen shows consistent importance dominance without crossover, indicating model dependence. We report both the paired median difference $\\Delta$ ($0.39 \\to 8.48$) as the primary metric and the amplification ratio $G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$ (importance) vs.\\ $G \\approx 1$ (random) as an auxiliary measure; $G$ is amplified by near-zero $\\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high}}$ denominators, but $\\Delta$---which involves no ratios---shows the same monotonic gradient (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\nWe show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (500 MATH-500 problems), the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$; Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}33.7$, $p \\ll 10^{-10}$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.07 \\to 3.16$; all $p < 10^{-14}$); preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) suggests the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 but at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with substantially weaker strategy contrast.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$ ($3$--$17{\\times}$ smaller than importance-based where both strategies are significant, $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.\n\nWe present preliminary correlational evidence for \\emph{importance score flattening}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a contributing factor, with the \\emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning (which selectively removes high-score heads) as a key differentiator from random pruning.\n\nThis work is a \\textbf{diagnostic study}: we characterize a previously unknown interaction effect rather than proposing a mitigation method. Following the tradition of empirical analysis papers in NLP \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}, our contribution is establishing and quantifying the phenomenon of phase-modulated compound severity, providing measurement tools and diagnostic findings that inform future method design.\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is $3$--$17{\\times}$ larger than under random eviction where both strategies show significant phase gradients ($s \\geq 0.25$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}).\\footnote{The full range including non-significant random conditions ($s \\leq 0.20$, where random $\\Delta$ CIs span zero) extends to $39{\\times}$.}\n    \\item We show this gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two Qwen2-architecture models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), with exploratory evidence from a LlamaForCausalLM model suggesting the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n    \\item We provide a \\textbf{diagnostic analysis} revealing that aggregate score collapse (Gini) is necessary but not sufficient for severity amplification, pointing to structural specificity of pruned heads as the key differentiator (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "oldString": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}",
    "newString": "\\section{Importance Score Flattening under Compound Sparsity}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe mechanisms discussed here are \\emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. We present discrimination collapse as one such diagnostic rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \\emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{Gini}} \\approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071\n\n\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nHead pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \\emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071\n\nThis pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nTo provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity\n\nWe note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nAs a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

    "oldString": "\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}",
    "newString": "\\subsection{Importance Score Flattening}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Importance Score Flattening under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe mechanisms discussed here are \\emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. We present importance score flattening as one such diagnostic rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \\emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{Gini}} \\approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071\n\n\\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nHead pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \\emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071\n\nThis pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nTo provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity\n\nWe note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nAs a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

  "uuid": "1da63241-b094-47d1-b214-a9a1b59be567",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "This pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with",
    "newString": "This pattern---\\emph{importance score flattening}---is consistent with",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Importance Score Flattening under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe mechanisms discussed here are \\emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. We present importance score flattening as one such diagnostic rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \\emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{Gini}} \\approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071\n\n\\subsection{Importance Score Flattening}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nHead pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that importance score flattening is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \\emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071\n\nThis pattern---which we term \\emph{discrimination collapse}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nTo provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity\n\nWe note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nAs a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "Discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence.",
    "newString": "Importance score flattening remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Importance Score Flattening under Compound Sparsity}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe mechanisms discussed here are \\emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. We present importance score flattening as one such diagnostic rather than a complete causal explanation. The consistent Gini decrease across all conditions provides a necessary background---aggregate attention concentration drops uniformly under head pruning regardless of eviction strategy (importance $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.030$ to $-0.081$; random $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; ${<}10\\%$ difference). However, severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$ between strategies despite near-identical Gini changes, indicating that concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \\emph{structural specificity}: importance-based pruning systematically removes high-attention-score heads, disrupting the attention pattern structure that KV eviction relies on, while random pruning preserves more structural diversity. The quantitative gap between score-level changes ($6$--$16\\%$ relative Gini reduction from a DeepSeek baseline of $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{Gini}} \\approx 0.50$; Qwen baseline $\\approx 0.57$) and outcome-level amplification ($G{=}7.5$--$79.8{\\times}$) indicates additional nonlinear factors (e.g., cascading effects across layers) that warrant dedicated investigation.  % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS baseline Gini 0.496, QW 0.571; imp ΔGini -0.029 to -0.082, rand -0.031 to -0.071\n\n\\subsection{Importance Score Flattening}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}\n\nHead pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Critically, random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops at matched sparsity (DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: importance $-0.065$ vs.\\ random $-0.060$; $s{=}0.30$: $-0.081$ vs.\\ $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$ for both strategies), confirming that importance score flattening is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not the eviction strategy. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\\times}$ larger than random at matched sparsity---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \\emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades, while random eviction's decisions are score-independent. % exp_id: c4_entropy; DS imp vs rand ΔGini: s025 -0.065 vs -0.060, s030 -0.081 vs -0.071\n\nThis pattern---\\emph{importance score flattening}---is consistent with importance-based eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection when score distributions flatten. The effect is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. This observation is independently corroborated by \\cite{hies-2025}, who report importance score collapse under heavy single-axis pruning; our results extend this to the compound setting, where flattening is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\nTo provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). We acknowledge that the Frobenius norm and Gini coefficient share sensitivity to attention weight magnitude, making their correlation partially expected; the key evidence is the consistent large effect size ($r > 0.6$) indicating strong structural separation between pruned and retained head sets. % exp_id: w4_structural_specificity\n\nWe note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}\n\nAs a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",
