    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe showed that compound sparsity severity---the super-additive interaction between head pruning and KV cache eviction---is not phase-invariant but exhibits an emergent phase-strategy interaction spanning over an order of magnitude within a single generation ($n{=}496$/$475$ problems). Under importance-based eviction, the amplification ratio $G$ ranges from $7.5{\\times}$ to $79.8{\\times}$ across sparsity levels, with paired median differences $\\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (DeepSeek) and $0.07$ to $3.16$ (Qwen), all $p < 10^{-13}$. Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$, but $3$--$17{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based where both strategies are significant ($s \\geq 0.25$). A crossover on DeepSeek at $s{=}0.25$---where importance-based eviction reverses ranking across phases ($2.6{\\times}$ reversal)---demonstrates that no single strategy dominates uniformly; Qwen does not exhibit this crossover, indicating model dependence. The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at all four sparsity levels; suggestive preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) indicates the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2, though at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with a weaker strategy contrast. Discrimination collapse provides a correlational diagnostic supported by both Qwen2-based models, with structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator from random eviction---despite near-identical Gini drops ($<10\\%$ difference), severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$.\nThese findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a prerequisite and diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies. Extending this analysis to code reasoning (HumanEval, LiveCodeBench) and science reasoning (GPQA), validating the discrimination collapse mechanism through causal intervention, and characterizing asymmetric compound interactions are promising directions for future work.\n",

          " ",
          " We showed that compound sparsity severity---the super-additive interaction between head pruning and KV cache eviction---is not phase-invariant but exhibits an emergent phase-strategy interaction spanning over an order of magnitude within a single generation ($n{=}496$/$475$ problems). Under importance-based eviction, the amplification ratio $G$ ranges from $7.5{\\times}$ to $79.8{\\times}$ across sparsity levels, with paired median differences $\\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (DeepSeek) and $0.07$ to $3.16$ (Qwen), all $p < 10^{-13}$. Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$, but $3$--$17{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based where both strategies are significant ($s \\geq 0.25$). A crossover on DeepSeek at $s{=}0.25$---where importance-based eviction reverses ranking across phases ($2.6{\\times}$ reversal)---demonstrates that no single strategy dominates uniformly; Qwen does not exhibit this crossover, indicating model dependence. The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at all four sparsity levels; suggestive preliminary evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM) indicates the phenomenon may extend beyond Qwen2, though at ${\\sim}1/40$ the magnitude and with a weaker strategy contrast. Importance score flattening provides a correlational diagnostic supported by both Qwen2-based models, with structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator from random eviction---despite near-identical Gini drops ($<10\\%$ difference), severity diverges by $15$--$17{\\times}$.",
          "-These findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a prerequisite and diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies. Extending this analysis to code reasoning (HumanEval, LiveCodeBench) and science reasoning (GPQA), validating the discrimination collapse mechanism through causal intervention, and characterizing asymmetric compound interactions are promising directions for future work.",
          "+These findings establish the phase-dependent nature of compound severity as a prerequisite and diagnostic foundation for future phase-aware mitigation strategies. Extending this analysis to code reasoning (HumanEval, LiveCodeBench) and science reasoning (GPQA), validating the importance score flattening mechanism through causal intervention, and characterizing asymmetric compound interactions are promising directions for future work."
        ]
      }
    ],

    "originalFile": "\\paragraph{Model scale and architecture.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7--8B parameter scale. Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested. Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) partially confirms the qualitative pattern beyond the Qwen2 architecture---monotonically increasing $d_z$ with within-importance phase gradient $\\sim$1.0---but with substantially weaker interaction magnitude ($\\sim$1/40 of DeepSeek) and mixed statistical evidence (only $s{=}0.30$ significant after Bonferroni correction). The pattern has been tested on two architecture families (Qwen2ForCausalLM, LlamaForCausalLM); comprehensive validation across more diverse architectures (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention) and larger scales is needed for full generalization claims.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark. MATH-500 is a suitable first testbed because it elicits structured reasoning with clear phase transitions (exploratory vs.\\ structured computation), supports per-problem evaluation enabling paired statistical tests, and spans sufficient difficulty to produce meaningful variation in sequence length and attention dynamics. However, different reasoning tasks---code generation (e.g., HumanEval, LiveCodeBench), science reasoning (e.g., GPQA), multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile. Whether the phase-strategy interaction documented here extends to these domains is an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric sparsity assumption.}\nOur analysis applies symmetric sparsity rates: the same fraction $s$ is used for both head pruning and KV cache eviction. Practical deployments may use asymmetric budgets (e.g., aggressive head pruning with conservative cache eviction, or vice versa). The interaction dynamics under asymmetric compound sparsity may differ from the symmetric case studied here; investigating asymmetric compound interactions is a natural extension.\n\n\\paragraph{Mechanism.}\nThe discrimination collapse mechanism is supported by correlational evidence (consistent Gini/CV reduction across all conditions); causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation is needed. The entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2/4 Qwen conditions, suggesting model-specific factors.\n\n\\paragraph{Perplexity vs.\\ accuracy.}\nOur severity metric is perplexity-based (II), which captures reasoning process quality through next-token prediction degradation. We chose perplexity because its per-token granularity enables the phase-level decomposition central to our analysis; accuracy is binary per-problem and cannot be meaningfully decomposed by reasoning phase. Whether the PPL-level severity gradient translates to downstream accuracy requires future validation through generation under compound sparsity conditions. Even if final answers survive due to natural phase shielding (\\S\\ref{sec:answer-region}), degraded intermediate reasoning may produce silent reasoning errors, affect reliability on harder problems, reduce interpretability, and impact open-ended reasoning tasks where no structured answer format provides natural shielding.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics. We do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization. Extended limitations in Appendix~\\ref{app:limitations}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Compound compression.}\nCombining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.\n\\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \\cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.\n\\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.\n\\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.\nGRASPrune~\\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.\nPM-KVQ~\\cite{pmkvq-iclr2026} demonstrates that progressive quantization---gradually reducing bit-width over the generation process---mitigates cumulative error in long-CoT reasoning, implicitly introducing a temporal dimension to compression severity that resonates with our finding that compound interaction is phase-dependent.\n% TODO: ThinK (channel-level compound compression), Prune-then-Quantize (compression order interaction) citations pending Agent verification\nThese works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.\n\n\\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}\nMost methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.\nAda-KV~\\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \\cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.\n\\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.\nRetentiveKV~\\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.\n\\cite{hies-2025} independently show that importance-based head discrimination collapses under heavy single-axis pruning. Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.\nRLKV~\\cite{rlkv-2025} uses reinforcement learning to identify reasoning-critical heads and selectively preserve their KV caches, demonstrating that head-level importance varies significantly for reasoning tasks. LongFlow~\\cite{longflow-2026} targets the long-output setting of reasoning models with an efficient importance metric derived from attention intermediates.\nLethe~\\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.\nMUSTAFAR~\\cite{mustafar-neurips2025} applies multi-stage adaptive compression for reasoning, while LKV~\\cite{lkv-2026} learns end-to-end head-wise budgets for KV cache allocation.\nConcurrent work examines KV cache compression's impact on reasoning capabilities \\citep{hold-onto-thought-neurips2025}, evaluating single-axis compression strategies on mathematical reasoning benchmarks; our work extends this analysis to the compound setting where head pruning and KV eviction interact.\n% TODO: ForesightKV (reasoning-specific learned importance) citation pending Agent verification\nThese methods each optimize a single pruning axis---whether aggregation, metric, temporal, or channel---without characterizing compound interactions when multiple mechanisms are simultaneously active.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}\nThinKV~\\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.\n\\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\\ ``decisive'' representation phases.\n\\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.\n\\cite{physics-kvcache-2026} identify a sharp phase transition in single-axis KV cache compression at high sparsity, characterizing the single-dimension failure mode that our compound analysis extends across multiple axes.\nR-KV~\\cite{rkv-neurips2025} exploits reasoning-specific redundancy for aggressive cache compression, targeting per-axis optimization rather than characterizing compound degradation.\nThese works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.\nOur work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.\n",

Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that collapse is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.",

Our work extends this to the compound setting, revealing that flattening is amplified by the interaction of two compression axes and modulated by reasoning phase---phenomena absent in single-axis analysis. The crossover effect (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}) is a prediction that single-axis frameworks cannot generate.",
          " RLKV~\\cite{rlkv-2025} uses reinforcement learning to identify reasoning-critical heads and selectively preserve their KV caches, demonstrating that head-level importance varies significantly for reasoning tasks.           " Lethe~\\cite{lethe-aaai2026} introduces temporal adaptivity with layer-wise multi-round pruning, and SparK~\\cite{spark-aaai2026} extends eviction to the channel dimension with recoverable sparsity.",
          " MUSTAFAR~\\cite{mustafar-neurips2025} applies multi-stage adaptive compression for reasoning, while LKV~\\cite{lkv-2026} learns end-to-end head-wise budgets for KV cache allocation."
        ]

          " \\label{app:crossover-details}",
          " ",
          "-On DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is confirmed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse}). Qwen does not exhibit a crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases across all sparsity levels (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\\ $1.769$). % exp_id: n500_unified_final, table2_n500_full.csv",
          "+On DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \\emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\\ $1.113$) but \\emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is confirmed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}).           " We computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, confirming that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} - \\Delta_{\\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \\geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is confirmed, but the strategy$\\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication. % exp_id: c3_llama; power analysis",
          " ",
          " ",

          " ",
          " \\subsection{Discrimination Collapse}"
        ]

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "fa43022d-0273-4ed6-b4c8-b58815feb7bd",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "06cc4077-8afe-434b-aceb-69b968484fc9",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "d5a6f331-e092-49dd-969a-76073e3392df",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/docs/paper/appendix_disc_analysis.tex",
    "oldString": "establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity",
    "newString": "establishes importance score flattening as a robust consequence of compound sparsity",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases. At $s{=}0.25$, oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery ($p = 0.024$), but 95\\% CIs for both conditions span zero. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

    "oldString": "confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning",
    "newString": "confirming that importance score flattening is a consequence of head pruning",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes importance score flattening as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases. At $s{=}0.25$, oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery ($p = 0.024$), but 95\\% CIs for both conditions span zero. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Importance Score Flattening Details}\n\\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}\n\nThis cross-model consistency (Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes importance score flattening as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.071$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.046$), confirming that importance score flattening is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\n\\section{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended-discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}\nThe compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \\leq 0.30$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053\n\n\\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \\approx 0.20$: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ for both models, but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (DS: $\\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$; QW: $\\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $3$--$39{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based, confirming that the dominant amplification is strategy-specific. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; DS rand s025 0.257 p=0.006, s030 0.510 p=0.006; QW rand s025 0.393, s030 0.913\n\n\\paragraph{Entropy mechanism boundary conditions.}\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\n\n\n\\section{Pilot Mitigation Experiment}\n\\label{app:pilot-mitigation}\n\nWe explored oracle phase-aware budget reallocation, shifting sparsity budget from high-severity low-sparsity phases to low-severity high-sparsity phases. At $s{=}0.25$, oracle recovery---the fraction of compound interaction eliminated by phase-aware allocation---reached 18\\% (Wilcoxon $p = 0.002$), below the pre-registered 30\\% gate for claiming practical mitigation. A heuristic variant achieved 32\\% recovery ($p = 0.024$), but 95\\% CIs for both conditions span zero. These results indicate that phase-aware budget reallocation alone is insufficient to neutralize the severity gradient; effective mitigation likely requires joint optimization of budget allocation and eviction strategy selection across phases.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-training-paradigm replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) establishes that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, though both models share the same Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}, Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-arch}) shows qualitatively consistent within-importance phase gradients ($d_z \\sim 1.0$) but a substantially weaker strategy $\\times$ phase interaction than in Qwen2-based architectures; random pruning also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient on LLaMA, reducing the strategy contrast. This partial confirmation ($s{=}0.30$ Wilcoxon $p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$; $s \\leq 0.25$ non-significant) suggests the qualitative pattern may generalize but quantitative impact is architecture-dependent. Comprehensive cross-architecture validation across more diverse designs (e.g., Mistral, multi-latent attention architectures) and scales remains an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples (seed$=$42). The primary analysis uses $n{=}496$ paired problems for DeepSeek (4 excluded) and $n{=}475$ for Qwen (25 excluded); the permutation test (Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) uses $n{=}49$ from an initial profiling subset. % exp_id: n500_unified_final; deepseek_bf16_permtest\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratios up to $43{:}1$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: n500_unified_final\nAll four profiling-subset permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. At $n{=}496$, Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm all conditions with $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nAt $n{=}500$, random eviction on both models becomes significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ (Qwen: $\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ at $s{=}0.25$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$ at $s{=}0.30$; bootstrap CIs exclude zero). % exp_id: n500_unified_final; QW rand s025 [0.236, 0.553], s030 [0.613, 1.258]\nThese effects are genuine but $2.7$--$20{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based eviction at the same sparsity levels, consistent with a weak phase gradient that is detectable at sufficient sample size but not the primary driver of compound severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency, which are necessary for deployment analysis but orthogonal to our severity characterization.\n\n% Entropy redistribution analysis moved to main text \\S\\ref{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n",
